Optimal and Robust Category-level Perception: Object Pose and Shape Estimation from 2D and 3D Semantic Keypoints
Jingnan Shi, Heng Yang, Luca Carlone

Abstract
We consider a category-level perception problem, where one is given 2D or 3D sensor data picturing an object of a given category ( e.g., a car), and has to reconstruct the 3D pose and shape of the object despite intra-class variability ( i.e., different car models have different shapes). We consider an active shape model , where —for an object category— we are given a library of potential CAD models describing objects in that category, and we adopt a standard formulation where pose and shape are estimated from 2D or 3D keypoints via non-convex optimization. Our first contribution is to develop PACE3D ⋆ and PACE2D ⋆ , the first certifiably optimal solvers for pose and shape estimation using 3D and 2D keypoints, respectively. Both solvers rely on the design of tight ( i.e., exact) semidefinite relaxations. Our second contribution is to develop outlier-robust versions of both solvers, named PACE3D# and PACE2D# . Towards this goal, we propose ROBIN , a general graph-theoretic framework to prune outliers, which uses compatibility hypergraphs to model measurements’ compatibility. We show that in category-level perception problems these hypergraphs can be built from the winding orders of the keypoints (in 2D) or their convex hulls (in 3D), and many outliers can be filtered out via maximum hyperclique computation. The last contribution is an extensive experimental evaluation. Besides providing an ablation study on simulated datasets and on the PASCAL3D+ dataset, we combine our solver with a deep keypoint detector, and show that PACE3D# improves over the state of the art in vehicle pose estimation in the ApolloScape datasets, and its runtime is compatible with practical applications. We release our code at https://github.com/MIT-SPARK/PACE .



This paper has been accepted for publication at the IEEE Transactions on Robotics, 2023.

Please cite the paper as: J. Shi, H. Yang, and L. Carlone, “Optimal and Robust Category-level Perception: Object Pose

and Shape Estimation from 2D and 3D Semantic Keypoints,”
IEEE Trans. Robotics
, 2023.

I
Introduction

Robotics applications, from self-driving cars to domestic robotics, demand
robots to be able to identify and estimate the
pose and shape of objects in the environment.
The perception system of a self-driving car needs to
estimate the poses of other vehicles, identify traffic lights and signs, and
detect pedestrians. Similarly, domestic applications require estimating the location and shape of objects to support effective interaction and manipulation
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
.
Object pose estimation is made harder by the large intra-class shape variability of common objects:
for instance, the shape of a car largely varies depending on the model (
e.g.,
take a van versus a sedan).

Despite the fast-paced progress, reliable 3D object pose estimation remains a challenge,
as witnessed by recent self-driving car accidents caused by
misdetections
[
4
]
. Deep learning has been making great strides in enabling robots to detect objects;
popular tools such as YOLO
[
5
]
and Mask-RCNN
[
6
]
have made object detection possible on commodity hardware and with reasonable performance for in-distribution test data.
However, detections are typically at the level of
categories (
e.g.,
car) rather than

at the level of instances (
e.g.,
a specific car model).
In turn, category-level perception renders the use of standard tools for pose estimation (from point cloud registration
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
to the Perspective-n-Point problem
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
) ineffective, since they rely on the knowledge of the shape of the object.

These limitations have triggered robotics and computer vision
research on category-level 3D object pose and shape estimation (see Section
IX
for an in-depth review).
Traditional methods include the popular

active shape model

[
13
,
14
,
15
]
, where
one attempts to estimate the pose and shape of an object given a large database of 3D CAD models.
Despite its popularity (
e.g.,
the model is also used in human shape estimation and face detection
[
14
]
),
estimation with active shape models leads to a non-convex optimization
problem and local solvers get stuck in poor solutions, and are sensitive to outliers
[
14
,
15
]
.
More recently, research effort has been devoted to end-to-end learning-based 3D pose estimation
with encouraging results in human pose estimation
[
16
]
and vehicle pose estimation
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
;
these approaches still require a large amount of 3D labeled data,
which is time-consuming (or expensive) to obtain in the wild.

Contribution.

We address the shortcomings of existing approaches for pose and shape estimation based on the active shape model and
propose the first approaches that can compute optimal estimates and are robust to outliers.
We consider a
category-level perception
problem, where one is given
keypoint detections of an object belonging to a given category (
e.g.,
detections of the wheels, rear-view mirrors, and other interest points of a car), and has to reconstruct
the pose and shape of the object.
We assume the availability of
a library of CAD models of objects in that category;
such a library is typically available, since CAD models are extensively used in the design, manufacturing, and simulation of 3D objects.

Note that the definition of category-level perception is ambiguous in the literature:
we follow the setup in
[
22
,
1
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
2
]
, while some authors use the same name for problems
where the objects seen at test-time are different from the ones seen during training
[
26
]
, while being in the same category.

Our first contribution is to develop the first
certifiably optimal
solvers for pose and shape estimation using 3D and 2D keypoints. In the 3D case, we show that —despite the non-convexity of the problem— rotation estimation can be decoupled from the estimation of object translation and shape, and
we demonstrate that (i) the optimal object rotation can be computed via a tight (small-size) semidefinite relaxation,
and (ii) the translation and shape parameters can be computed in closed form given the rotation.
We call the resulting solver
PACE3D
⋆
(
Pose and shApe estimation for 3D-3D Category-level pErception
).
In the 2D case, we formulate pose and shape estimation using an algebraic point-to-line cost,
and leverage
Lasserre’s hierarchy of semidefinite relaxations

[
27
]
to solve the
problem to certifiable global optimality.
We call the resulting solver
PACE2D
⋆
(
Pose and shApe estimation for 2D-3D Category-level pErception
).
Contrarily to
PACE3D
⋆
,
PACE2D
⋆
leads to semidefinite relaxations whose size increases with the
number of CAD models in the active shape model.

Our second contribution is to develop an outlier rejection scheme applicable to both
PACE3D
⋆
and
PACE2D
⋆
.
Towards this goal, we introduce a general framework for graph-theoretic outlier pruning, named
ROBIN
, which
generalizes our previous work
[
7
,
28
]
to use hypergraphs.

ROBIN
models compatibility between subset of measurements
using a
compatibility hypergraph
.
We show that the compatibility hypergraph can be efficiently constructed by inspecting the
winding orders of the keypoints in 2D, or the convex hulls of the keypoints in 3D.
We then prove that all the inliers are contained in a single hyperclique of
the compatibility hypergraph and can be typically found within the maximum hyperclique.

ROBIN
is able to remove a large fraction of outliers. The resulting measurements are then passed to our optimal solvers (
PACE3D
⋆
and
PACE2D
⋆
), that we also wrap in
a standard
graduated non-convexity

[
29
]
scheme
to mitigate the impact of outliers that survived
ROBIN
.
The resulting outlier-robust approaches are named
PACE3D#
and
PACE2D#
and are illustrated in Fig.
.

Our last contribution is an extensive experimental evaluation in both synthetic experiments and real datasets
[
30
]
. We provide an ablation study on
simulated datasets and on the
PASCAL3D+
dataset, and show that
(i)
PACE3D
⋆
is more accurate than state-of-the-art iterative solvers,
(ii)
PACE2D
⋆
is more accurate than baseline local solvers and convex relaxations based on the weak perspective projection model
[
14
,
15
]
,
(iii)
PACE3D#
dominates other robust solvers and is robust to
\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
outliers,
and (iv)
PACE2D#
is robust to
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\%
outliers. Finally, we integrate our solvers in a realistic system —including a deep keypoint detector— and apply it to vehicle pose and shape estimation in the
ApolloScape

[
30
]
driving datasets.
While
PACE2D#
is currently slow and suffers from the low quality of the deep keypoint detections,

PACE3D#
largely outperforms the state of the art and a non-optimized implementation runs in a fraction of a second. We also show that
ROBIN
is even able to detect mislabeled keypoints
used to train the keypoint detector in the
ApolloScape
dataset
[
30
]
.

Novelty with Respect to
[
28
,
31
]
.

In our previous works,
we introduced
ROBIN

[
28
]
, a graph theoretic outlier rejection framework,
and two solvers
[
31
]
(
PACE3D
⋆
and
PACE3D#
) for pose and shape estimation from 3D keypoints.
The present paper (i) allows
ROBIN
to handle more general compatibility tests, and
(ii) extends the concept of inlier selection to maximum hypercliques on
compatibility hypergraphs
.
This paper also develops

PACE2D
⋆
and
PACE2D#
, which estimate pose and shape from only 2D (instead of 3D) keypoints.
In addition, we report a more comprehensive experimental evaluation in simulation and on the
ApolloScape
dataset.

Paper Structure.

Section
II
formulates the category-level perception problem.
Section
III
provides a brief overview of the proposed approaches (also summarized in Fig.
).
Sections
IV
and
V
present our graph-theoretic outlier
pruning (
ROBIN
) and its application to category-level perception.
Section
VI
introduces our certifiably optimal solvers for category-level perception
and Section
VII
recalls how to wrap the solvers in a graduated non-convexity scheme.
Section
VIII
discusses experimental results.
Section
IX
provides an in-depth review of related work.
Section
X
concludes the paper.

Illustration of a simple active shape model with two CAD models: a Hyundai Sonata (with shape coefficient
\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28721}
) and an Audi Q7 (with shape coefficient
\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28722}
).
From left to right, we show four convex combinations of the CAD models, with

II
Problem Statement:

3D-3D and 2D-3D Category-Level Perception

In this section, we formulate the 3D-3D and 2D-3D category-level perception problems.
The goal is to compute the 3D pose and shape of an object, given 3D or 2D sensor data.
We focus on a
multi-stage
setup (
cf.
Section
IX
) where a front-end is used to extract 2D or 3D semantic keypoints from the sensor data,
which are then used by a back-end solver to estimate the object’s 3D pose
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\delimiter 84054785
and shape,
where
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 12850\mathrm{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 28723}}
are the 3D rotation and translation, respectively.
The front-end is typically implemented using standard deep networks
[
3
,
32
]
, while our goal here is to design more accurate and robust back-ends.

In the following, we first introduce a standard parametrization of the object shape (Section
II-A
),
and then formalize the 3D-3D category-level perception problem (Section
II-B
)
and its 2D-3D counterpart (Section
II-C
).

II-A

Active Shape Model

We assume the object shape to be partially specified: we are given a library
of 3D CAD models
{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 29035}
,
\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29003
, and assume that the unknown object shape
{\cal\mathchar 29011}

(modeled as a collection of 3D points) can be written
as a combination of the points on the given CAD models.
More formally,
each point
{\bm{\mathchar 29043}}_{\mathchar 29033}
of the
shape
{\cal\mathchar 29011}
can be written as:

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathchar 29043}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\textstyle\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}

(1)

where
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
is a given point on the surface of the CAD model
{\cal\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 29035}
;
the
shape parameters

{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\triangleq\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 28721}\ldots\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 84267779^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
are unknown, and
the entries of
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
are assumed to be non-negative and sum up to 1,

i.e.,

{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
belongs to the
\mathchar 29003
-dimensional probability simplex
\mathchar 28673_{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\{{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29003}}\mathchar 12906{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12821{\mathbf{\mathchar 28720}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\}
.
For instance, if —upon estimation—
the vector
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
has the
\mathchar 29036
-th entry equal to 1 and the remaining entries equal to zero in (
1
), then the
estimated shape matches the
\mathchar 29036
-th CAD model in the library; therefore, the estimation of the
shape parameters
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
can be understood as a fine-grained classification of the object among the instances in the library. However, the model is even more expressive, since it allows the object shape to be a convex
combination of CAD models, which enables the active shape model (
1
) to interpolate between different shapes in the library; see Fig.
1
.

II-B

3D-3D Category-Level Perception

In the 3D-3D category-level perception problem, the goal is to
estimate an object’s pose and shape, given a set of
\mathchar 29006
3D keypoint detections, typically obtained
using learning-based keypoint detectors.
Such detectors are
trained to detect semantic features of the 3D object (
e.g.,
wheels of a car),
and can be
applied to RGB-D or RGB+Lidar data (
e.g.,

[
3
]
).

We assume each
3D measurement

{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
(
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006
) is a noisy measurement of a keypoint
{\bm{\mathchar 29043}}_{\mathchar 29033}

of our target object, in the coordinate frame of the sensor.
More formally, each
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
is described by the following generative model:

{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\textstyle\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\qquad\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006

(2)

where the measurement
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
pictures a 3D point on the object (written as a linear combination
\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
of the shapes in the library as in (
1
)), after the point is rotated and translated
according to the 3D pose
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\delimiter 84054785
of the object, and where
{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
represents measurement noise.
Intuitively, each measurement corresponds to a noisy measurement of a semantic feature of the object
(
e.g.,
wheel center or rear-view mirrors of a car) and each
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
corresponds to the corresponding feature (
e.g.,
wheel or mirror) location for a specific CAD model.

Problem 1 (Robust 3D-3D Category-Level Perception).

Compute the 3D pose
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\delimiter 84054785
and shape
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\delimiter 84054785
of an object given
\mathchar 29006

form (
2

i.e.,
measurements with large error
{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
.

III
Overview of
PACE#
: Pose and Shape Estimation for Robust Category-level Perception

Our approach, named
PACE#
,
is summarized in Fig.
, for both the 3D-3D case
(
PACE3D#
, Fig.
(a)) and the 2D-3D case (
PACE2D#
, Fig.
(b)).
We assume access to a perception front-end that detects semantic keypoints given sensor data.
Our work forms the back-end, and consists of two stages.
In the first stage,
we employ a graph-theoretic framework, named
ROBIN
, to pre-process the keypoints and
prune gross outliers without explicitly solving the underlying estimation problem.
We then pass the filtered measurements to the second stage,
where an optimal solver (wrapped in a graduated non-convexity scheme) computes a pose and shape estimate.
2
2

2

We use a two-stage approach (
i.e.,

ROBIN
followed by graduated non-convexity) for two reasons.
While our solver with graduated non-convexity is robust against
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\%
of outliers in 2D-3D problems and
\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720\%
of outliers in 3D-3D problems,
our goal is to further increase its robustness. Indeed we show that
ROBIN
boosts robustness to
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\%
in 2D-3D problems and
\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28720\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28720\%
in 3D-3D problems.
In addition, since the first stage prunes outliers independently from the solver,

ROBIN
can be used in a plug-and-play manner with other existing solvers to boost their robustness.

In the following we introduce Stage 1 by first presenting
ROBIN
, a general framework for outlier pruning (Section
IV
)
and then discussing its application to category-level perception (Section
V
);
we then discuss Stage 2
by presenting our optimal solvers for 3D-3D (
PACE3D
⋆
, Section
VI-A
) and 2D-3D (
PACE2D
⋆
, Section
VI-B
) category-level perception, and a brief review of graduated non-convexity
[
29
]
(Section
VII
).

IV
Stage 1: Graph-theoretic Outlier Pruning With
ROBIN

This section develops a general framework to prune gross outliers from a set of measurements without
explicitly computing an estimate for the variables of interest.
In particular, we introduce the notion of
\mathchar 29038
-invariant
to check if a subset of measurements contains outliers.
We then use these checks to construct

compatibility hypergraphs
that describe mutually compatible measurements,
and show how to reject outliers by computing maximum hypercliques of these graphs.
Combining these insights, we obtain

ROBIN
(
Reject Outliers Based on INvariants
), our graph-theoretic algorithm for pruning outliers.

This section presents our framework in full generality and then we tailor it to category-level perception in Section
V
.
In particular, here we consider a more general measurement model
that relates measurements
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{\mathchar 29033}
to
the to-be-estimated variable
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}
(where
\mathbb{\mathchar 29016}
is the domain of
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
,
e.g.,
the set of 3D poses) and a given model
{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}_{\mathchar 29033}
(
e.g.,
our CAD models):

{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29032}}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\qquad\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006

(4)

where
{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\mathchar 29033}
denotes the measurement noise.
Clearly, eqs. (
2
) and (
3
) can be understood as
special instances of (
4
), where
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
includes the unknown pose and shape of the
object, and the (given) model
{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}
corresponds to the CAD models.

IV-A

From Measurements to Invariants

This section formalizes the concepts of
\mathchar 29038
-invariant and generalized
\mathchar 29038
-invariant, which
are the building blocks of our outlier pruning framework.
The main motivation is to use invariance to establish checks on the (inlier) measurements that hold true
regardless of the state under estimation; we are later going to use these checks to detect outliers.

Let us consider the measurements in eq. (
4
) and
denote the indices of the measurements as
{\cal\mathchar 29017}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mathchar 61}\limits^{\textstyle\mathchar 314}}}\{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006\}
.
For a given integer
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29006
, let
{\cal\mathchar 29005}\mathchar 12826{\cal\mathchar 29017}
be a subset of
\mathchar 29038
indices in
{\cal\mathchar 29017}
, and denote with
{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29034}
the
\mathchar 29034
-th element of this subset (with
\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84054785
.
Then, we use the following notation:

{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{{\cal\mathchar 29005}}\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 67482370\begin{array}[]{cc}{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 28721}}\\
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 28722}}\\
\mathord{\vbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{.}\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\\
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 29038}}\end{array}\right\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 24891\;\;{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}_{{\cal\mathchar 29005}}\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 67482370\begin{array}[]{cc}{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}_{{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 28721}}\\
{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}_{{\cal\mathchar 29005}_{\mathchar 28722}}\\

Definition 2 (Generalized
\mathchar 29038

Given eq. (

Intuitively, because of the noise, now the measurements can produce a number of different invariants

IV-B

From Invariants to Compatibility Tests for Outlier Pruning

While the previous section developed invariants without distinguishing inliers from outliers, this section shows that
the resulting invariants can be directly used to check if a subset of measurements contains an outlier.
Towards this goal, we formalize the notion of inlier and outlier.

Definition 3 (Inliers and Outliers).

Given measurements (
4
) and a threshold
\mathchar 28940{\mathchar 12821}\mathchar 28720
,
a measurement
\mathchar 29033
is an
inlier
if the corresponding noise satisfies
\delimiter 69645069{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 12820\!\mathchar 28940

and is an
outlier
otherwise.

The notion of invariants introduced in the previous section allowed us to obtain relations that
depend on the measurements and a noise bound,
but are independent on
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}
, see eq. (
9
).
Therefore, we can directly use these relations to check if a subset of
\mathchar 29038
measurements contains outliers:
if eq. (
9
)
is not satisfied by a subset of measurements
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{{\cal\mathchar 29005}}

then the corresponding subset of measurements
must
contain an outlier.
We call the corresponding check a
compatibility test
.
In the following, we provide an example of compatibility test for point cloud registration.
The reader can find more examples of compatibility tests for other applications in
[
28
]
.

Example of Compatibility Test.

For our point cloud registration example,
eq. (
13
) states that
any pair of measurements
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{\mathchar 29033}
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{\mathchar 29034}

with noise
\delimiter 69645069{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 12820\!\mathchar 28940
and
\delimiter 69645069{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 12820\!\mathchar 28940

must satify:

\delimiter 69645069{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{\mathchar 29034}\!\mathchar 8704\!{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 12850\Big\delimiter 67482370\delimiter 69645069{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28940\mathchar 24891\delimiter 69645069{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 28946}}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28940\Big\delimiter 84267779

(14)

If the relation is satisfied, we say that
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{\mathchar 29033}
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{\mathchar 29034}
are compatible with each other
(
i.e.,
they can potentially be both inliers); however, if the relation is
not
satisfied, then one of the measurements
must
be an outlier.
Generalizing this example, we obtain the following general definition of
compatibility test
.

Definition 4 (Compatibility Test).

Given a subset of
\mathchar 29038
measurements and the corresponding
\mathchar 29038
-invariant, a compatibility test
is a binary condition (computed using the invariant), such that if the condition fails, then the set of measurements

must
contain at least one outlier.

Note that we require the test to be
sound
(
i.e.,
it does not detect outliers when testing a set of inliers), but
may not be
complete
(
i.e.,
the test might pass even in the presence of outliers).
This property is important since our goal is to prune as many outliers as we can, while preserving the inliers.
Also note that the test detects if the set contains outliers, but does not provide information on
which
measurements are outliers. We are going to fill in this gap below.

IV-C

From Compatibility Tests to Compatibility Hypergraph

For a problem with an
\mathchar 29038
-invariant,
we describe the results of the compatibility tests for all subsets of
\mathchar 29038
measurements using a

compatibility hypergraph
.

Definition 5 (Compatibility Hypergraph).

Given a compatibility test with
\mathchar 29038
measurements, define the compatibility hypergraph
{\cal\mathchar 28999}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29014}\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 28997}\delimiter 84054785
as
an
\mathchar 29038
-uniform undirected hypergraph,
3
3

3

In an
\mathchar 29038
-uniform hypergraph, each hyperedge involves exactly
\mathchar 29038
nodes.
where
each node
\delimiter 69640972
in the node set
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
is associated to a measurement in (
4
), and
an hyperedge
\mathchar 29029
(connecting a subset of
\mathchar 29038
measurements) belongs to
the edge set
{\cal\mathchar 28997}
if and only if its subset of measurements passes the compatibility test.

Note that in the case where
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
,
the above definition reduces to a regular undirected graph.
Building the compatibility graph requires looping over all subsets of
\mathchar 29038
measurements and, whenever the subset
passes the compatibility test, adding a hyperedge between the corresponding
\mathchar 29038
nodes in the graph.
Note that these checks are very fast and easy to parallelize since they only involve checking boolean conditions (
e.g.,
(
12
)) without computing an estimate (as opposed to
RANSAC
).

Inlier Structures in Compatibility Hypergraphs.

Here we show that the inliers in the set of measurements (
4
) are contained in a
single hyperclique of the compatibility hypergraph.
Let us start with some definitions.

Definition 6 (Hypercliques and Maximum Hyperclique in Hypergraphs).

A hyperclique of an
\mathchar 29038
-uniform hypergraph
{\cal\mathchar 28999}
is a set of vertices such that any subsets of
\mathchar 29038
vertices is connected by an hyperedge in
{\cal\mathchar 28999}
.
The
maximum
hyperclique of
{\cal\mathchar 28999}
is the hyperclique with the largest number of vertices.

Again, in the case where
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
, the above definition reduces to the usual clique and maximum clique definition.
Given a compatibility hypergraph
{\cal\mathchar 28999}
, the following result relates the set of inliers in (
4
)
with hypercliques in
{\cal\mathchar 28999}
(proof in App.
A
in
[
34
]
).

Theorem 7 (Inliers and Hypercliques).

Assume we are given measurements (
4
) (with inlier noise bound
\mathchar 28940
) and the corresponding
\mathchar 29038
-invariants;
call
{\cal\mathchar 28999}
the corresponding compatibility hypergraph.
Then, assuming there are at least
\mathchar 29038
inliers, all inliers belong to a single hyperclique in
{\cal\mathchar 28999}
.

Theorem
7
implies that we can look for inliers by computing hypercliques in the compatibility graph.
Since we expect to have more compatible inliers than outliers, here we propose to compute the maximum hyperclique;
this approach is shown to work extremely well in practice in Section
VIII
.
App.
C
in
[
34
]
describes an algorithm to find
the maximum hyperclique.

Comparison with
[
28
]
.

In our previous work
[
28
]
—where we first proposed
ROBIN
— we defined compatibility graphs as ordinary graphs, and inliers structures as maximum cliques.
In the present paper, we define compatibility graphs as hypergraphs (Definition
5
), and inlier structures as maximum hypercliques (Theorem
7
).
This new formulation is equivalent to
[
28
]
for
\mathchar 28722
-invariants, but different otherwise.
Topologically, the compatibility graphs in
[
28
]
can be seen as
clique-expanded
compatibility hypergraphs, where each hyperedge on a subset of
\mathchar 29038
nodes is substituted with pairwise edges between all nodes in the subset (
i.e.,
a clique in the graph).
Compared to
[
28
]
, our new formulation leads to pruning a larger number of outliers (see App.
B
in
[
34
]
for a concrete example).

.

Algorithm 1

ROBIN

IV-D

ROBIN
: Graph-theoretic Outlier Rejection

This section summarizes all the findings above into a single algorithm for graph-theoretic outlier pruning, named

ROBIN
(
Reject Outliers Based on INvariants
).

ROBIN
’s pseudocode is given in Algorithm
1
.
The algorithm takes a set of measurements
{\cal\mathchar 29017}
in input, and outputs a subset
{\cal\mathchar 29017}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12826{\cal\mathchar 29017}
from which many outliers have been pruned.
Given an
\mathchar 29038
-invariant,
ROBIN
first performs compatibility tests on all subsets of
\mathchar 29038
measurements
and builds the corresponding compatibility hypergraph (lines
1
-
1
).
Then, it uses a maximum hyperclique solver to compute the subset of measurements surviving outlier pruning (lines
1
-
1
).
We have implemented the maximum hyperclique solver described in App.
C
in
[
34
]
using CVXPY
[
35
]
;
in the special case where
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
,
i.e.,
the compatibility graph is an ordinary graph,
we use the parallel maximum clique solver from
[
36
]
.
We remark that
ROBIN
is not guaranteed to reject all outliers,
i.e.,
some outliers may still pass the compatibility tests and end up in the maximum hyperclique.
Indeed, as mentioned in the introduction,
ROBIN
is designed to be a preprocessing
step to prune gross outliers and enhance the robustness of existing robust estimators.
Note that Algorithm
1
can be applied to various estimation problems,
as long as suitable compatibility tests are defined (
i.e.,

testCompatibility
function).
In
[
28
]
, we report applications to many geometric perception problems, including point cloud registration,
point-with-normal registration, and camera pose estimation.
In the following section, we develop compatibility tests for 3D-3D and 2D-3D category-level perception problems.

V
Stage 1 (continued): Application to Category-Level Perception

This section tailors
ROBIN
to category-level perception.
Specifically, we develop
compatibility tests

for Problem
1
(Section
V-A
) and Problem
2
(Section
V-B
).

V-A

3D-3D Category-level Compatibility Test

We develop a 3D-3D category-level compatibility test to check if a pair of 3D keypoint measurements are mutually compatible; our test generalizes results
on instance-level perception, where the object shape is known
[
7
,
37
,
28
]
.

Fig. 2:
Example of compatibility test with 3 CAD models of cars (red, dark green, blue, indexed from 1 to 3). (Noiseless) inliers (
e.g.,
the detection of the back wheel

encompassing the back wheel positions across CAD models).
This restricts the relative distance between two inliers and allows filtering out outliers.
For instance, the dashed black line shows a distance that is compatible with the location of the convex hulls, while
the solid black line is too short compared to the relative position of the wheels (for any car model) and
allows pointing out that there is an outlier (

in the figure).

V-B

2D-3D Category-level Compatibility Test

The pairwise invariant presented in the previous section was inspired by the fact that the distance between
pairs of points is (a noiseless) invariant to rigid transformations. When it comes to 2D-3D problems, it is known that there is no
(noiseless) invariant for 3D points in generic configurations
under perspective projection
[
38
]
.
One option would be to use invariants for special configurations of points, such as cross ratios for collinear points
[
28
]
; however, this would not be generally applicable to our problem, where 3D keypoints are arbitrarily distributed on the CAD models.
Here, we take an alternative route and we directly design a generalized 3-invariant for
generic 3D point projections.

Our 2D-3D category-level compatibility test draws inspiration from back-face culling in computer graphics
[
39
]
.
The key idea is that when observing an object, the
winding order
of
triplet of keypoints seen in the image (roughly speaking: if the points are arranged in clockwise or counterclockwise order) must be consistent with the arrangement of the
corresponding triplet of keypoints in the CAD models.
Therefore,
we formulate a test by checking whether the observed winding order
matches our expectation from the CAD models.
In this section, we first define the notion of 2D and 3D winding orders,
as well as the visibility and covisibility regions (camera locations where triplets of points are covisible);
then we introduce a
\mathchar 28723
-invariant involving winding orders;
finally, we combine winding orders and covisibility regions to develop a 2D-3D category-level compatibility test.

Fig. 3:
Illustration of the concept of winding orders on a cube with three keypoints (in yellow) on its surface.
The cube is opaque, but shown as semi-transparent for visualization.
The plane cutting through all three keypoints (blue) has its normal
{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}
pointing outwards from the cube,
following eq. (
20
).
Camera 1 is in front of the plane, and the observed 2D winding order (Camera 1 View) is counterclockwise (enumerating in the order
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12833\mathchar 28722\mathchar 12833\mathchar 28723
),
following the right-hand rule with thumb pointing towards the viewer.
Camera 2 is behind the plane, and would observe a clockwise winding order.
The only feasible winding order is counterclockwise as observed by Camera 1, since the
keypoints would be occluded by the cube in Camera 2.

2D Winding Orders.

Winding orders refer to whether an ordered triplet of projected points are arranged in a clockwise or counterclockwise order.
For example, if we enumerate the points in Fig.
3
in ascending order of their indices (
i.e.,
1, 2, 3),
camera 1 sees points in counterclockwise order, while camera 2 sees them in clockwise order.

Definition 9 (2D Winding Order).

Given three 2D image points
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}
, and
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}
where
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29037
,
their winding order
is the orientation
{
clockwise, counterclockwise
}
of points
when enumerating them in the order
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12833\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12833\mathchar 29037
.

The following proposition allows computing the 2D winding order algebraically (proof in App.
E
in
[
34
]
).

Proposition 10 (2D Winding Order Computation).

Assume three non-collinear 2D image points
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}
, and
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}
,
then the
winding order
\mathchar 29015
can be computed as:

\displaystyle\mathchar 29015\mathchar 12349\begin{cases}\text{clockwise}&\text{if }\mathchar 29014\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720\\
\text{counterclockwise}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(19)

where
\mathchar 29014\mathchar 12349\det\left\delimiter 67273472\begin{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\;\;{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\end{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\right\delimiter 84054785
and

denotes the matrix determinant.

In words, Proposition

states that the 2D
winding order can be calculated from the signed area of the parallelogram formed by
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
.

Half-spaces and 3D Winding Orders.

While Proposition
10
provides a simple way to compute the winding order for
a triplet of 2D image points, towards our 2D-3D category-level invariant,
we need to establish a notion of winding order also for the 3D shape keypoints on a CAD model.
In the following we show that the winding order for a triplet of 3D points can be uniquely
determined given the location of the camera.
To develop some intuition, consider Fig.
3
, where we have three 3D keypoints on the faces of a cube.
The plane passing across the triplet of 3D keypoints divides the space into two half-spaces.
Theorem
11
below shows that, whenever the camera lies within one of the half-spaces, only one winding order is possible. Therefore, if the keypoints are only covisible by camera locations in one of the half-spaces, their winding order is uniquely determined.

Let us formally define the half-spaces induced by the triplet plane.
Let
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29034}
, and
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29037}
(
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29037
) be three model keypoints on the
\mathchar 29035
-th CAD model.
Define the triplet normal vector in the model’s frame as follows (
cf.

{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}
in Fig.
3
):

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}

\displaystyle\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8706\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785

(20)

So the triplet plane equation is:

\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8705{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
for any

If

(resp.
\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8705{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28720

{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}

In this section, one can think about

as the optical center of the camera in the CAD model’s frame, hence
the inequalities above capture which half-space the camera is located in.

The next theorem connects winding order with the two half-spaces created by the triplet plane (proof in App.

[

]
).

Theorem 11 (Half-spaces and 3D Winding Orders).

Under perspective projection per eq. (
3
) with zero noise (
{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
), the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\sgn{\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8705{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\delimiter 84054785}

\displaystyle\mathchar 8704\sgn{\left\delimiter 67273472\det\begin{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}&{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\end{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\right\delimiter 84054785}

(21)

where
\sgn\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84054785
is the signum function.

The theorem states that the half-space the camera is located in (identified by
\sgn{\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8705{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\delimiter 84054785}
)
uniquely determines the 2D winding order of the projection of the 3D points.
This is not informative if the camera can be anywhere, since both winding orders are possible.
In the following, we use the idea of covisibility regions to restrict potential
locations of the camera, such that we can associate a possibly unique winding order to triplets of 3D keypoints.

Fig. 4:
Visualization of 3D winding orders of three triplets of keypoints on the model
Q7-SUV
from the
ApolloScape
dataset.
(a) CAD model with keypoints.
Red points represent the selected keypoint triplets, while yellow planes indicate the planes formed by the triplets.
Arrows point towards the half-space in which the triplets are covisible.
Triplet (0, 1, 8) can be viewed in both clockwise and counterclockwise winding order in their covisibility region.
Triplet (12, 14, 17) can be only viewed in counterclockwise winding order.
Triplet (9, 39, 44) cannot be viewed in either winding order, as their covisibility region is empty.
(b-c) Winding orders and visibility of the triplets in a volume surrounding the car.
The top row (b) shows visibility of the keypoints on a slice along the xz-plane. Color-coded values represent how many keypoints are visible in the triplet; covisibility regions are shaded in yellow.
The bottom row (c) shows winding orders of the triplets in the covisibility region, with beige denoting counterclockwise, blue clockwise, and gray corresponding to cases where the covisibility region is empty.
The visibility region and winding order plots are generated following the ray tracing method described in App.
H
in
[
34
]
.

Visibility and Covisibility Regions.

Visibility and covisibility regions describe the set of camera locations from which keypoints are visible.
The 3D object observed by the camera is assumed to be opaque, hence a keypoint visible from one viewpoint, might not
be visible from another, due to self-occlusions.
Fig.
3
demonstrates this concept:
due to self-occlusions,
the three keypoints on the cube are visible to Camera 1, but occluded (
i.e.,
not visible) in Camera 2.

We now define the visibility region of keypoints on a shape,
following the standard definition
[
40
]
.

Definition 12 (Visibility Region).

The visibility region of a keypoint
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
is the
set of 3D points
\{{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\}
such that line segments connecting
{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
do not intersect the
\mathchar 29035
-th shape model.

We also define covisibility regions for keypoints triplets,
which are the 3D space in which all three keypoints are visible.

Definition 13 (Covisibility Region).

The covisibility region of a triplet of keypoints
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29034}
, and
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29037}
is the intersection
of the visibility region of each keypoint.

Fig.
4
(b) shows visibility and covisibility regions surrounding the
Q7-SUV
model from the
ApolloScape
dataset for selected triplets of keypoints.
For the triplet
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28728\delimiter 84054785
, their covisibility regions are to the front of the car,
whereas for
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28727\delimiter 84054785
, their covisibility regions are to the left of the car.
Notice how the relative positions between triplet half-spaces and covisibility regions affect the visible winding orders; see Fig.
4
(c).
The plane formed by
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28728\delimiter 84054785
cuts through their covisibility regions, hence both winding orders are visible.
For
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28727\delimiter 84054785
, their covisibility regions are on one side of the keypoints plane, so the only visible winding order is clockwise. This observation is formalized below.

Corollary 14 (Covisibility-constrained 3D Winding Orders).

The projection of a triplet of 3D keypoints
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29034}
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29037}
is arranged
in counterclockwise winding order if and only if

\displaystyle\{{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 28723}}\mathchar 12906\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8705{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 28995}\}\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\varnothing

(22)

where
\mathcal{\mathchar 28995}
is the covisibility region of
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29034}
, and
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29037}
on the
\mathchar 29035
-th shape.
Similarly, the keypoints can be viewed
in clockwise winding order if and only if

\displaystyle\{{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 28723}}\mathchar 12906\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8705{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 28995}\}\mathrel{{{{\mathchar 566\mathchar 61}}}}\varnothing

(23)

This corollary follows directly from Proposition
10
,
Theorem
11
, and Definition
13
.
Remarkably, the two feasibility problems in eqs. (
22
) and (
23
) and can be solved
a priori
, since they only depend on the
triplets of 3D keypoints and their normal.
For polyhedral shapes, the constraint
{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 28995}
can be expressed via linear constraints;
in addition, if we replace the strict inequalities in eqs. (
22
) and (
23
)
with non-strict ones and replace zeros with a small positive constant, the problems can be solved via linear programming.
For complex shapes, we can check the conditions in eqs. (
22
) and (
23
)
by splitting the space around the CAD models into voxels (
i.e.,
we discretize the set of possible
{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}
) and
ray tracing the keypoint to check visibility (see App.
H
in
[
34
]
).
In summary, given a CAD model and for each triplet of 3D keypoints, using eqs. (
22
) and (
23
) we are able to predict if a covisible triplet
will produce clockwise or counterclockwise keypoint projections.

For shape
\mathchar 29035
, its feasible winding order dictionary

(where

\{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8710\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 571\}

where
\{\sgn{\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\!\mathchar 8704\!{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\!\mathchar 8705\!{\bm{\mathchar 29038}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}\delimiter 84054785}\!\mathchar 12906\!\mathchar 568{\bm{\mathchar 29039}}\mathchar 12850\!\mathcal{\mathchar 28995}\}

We are now ready to define our generalized 3-invariant and the corresponding compatibility test.

2D-3D Invariant and Compatibility Test.

We solve the two feasibility problems (
22
) and (
23
)
for all triplets and CAD models and obtain a
dictionary of feasible winding orders
.
In essence, each dictionary serves as a compatibility test for a single shape:
for observed keypoints
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034}
, and
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{2D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037}
,
if the observed winding orders are not in
{\cal\mathchar 29015}_{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29033\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29034\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037\delimiter 84054785
, then the triplets are not compatible.
However, this dictionary is only for a single shape.
To formulate a 2D-3D category-level invariant,
we need to create a dictionary of feasible winding orders for all
\mathchar 29003
shapes.
We address this in Proposition
16
below.

Intuitively, the proposition states that the observed winding order must match at least one of the winding orders contained in the feasible winding order dictionary of all shapes.
The non-compatible triplets from Proposition
16

are points with noise so large that the measured winding orders become inconsistent with the models.
Under technical conditions (discussed in App.

in
[
34
]
), Proposition
16
holds true even when keypoints are generated by convex combinations of
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
.

VI
Stage II: Certifiably Optimal Solvers for Category-Level Perception

While Stage 1 serves the purpose of filtering out a large fraction of gross outliers
(without even computing an estimate),
Stage 2 aims to use the remaining measurements to estimate the pose and shape parameters.
In this section, we develop
certifiably optimal
solvers
for Problem
1
and
2
in the
outlier-free
case (
i.e.,
assuming that

ROBIN
removed all the outliers).
In Section
VII
,
we further improve robustness by incorporating a
graduated non-convexity
scheme to handle potential
left-over outliers in the measurements after
ROBIN
.

VI-A

Certifiably Optimal Solver for Outlier-free

3D-3D Category-Level Perception

We show how to solve Problem
1
in the outlier-free case, where
the noise
{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
is assumed to follow a zero-mean Gaussian distribution.
In the outlier-free case, a standard formulation for the pose and shape estimation problem leads to a

regularized non-linear least squares
problem:

\hskip-14.22636pt\min_{\begin{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}{\mathchar29027}{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 12850\mathrm{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\\
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 28723}}\mathchar 24891{\mathbf{\mathchar 28721}}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\end{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}}\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28949\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}

(3D-3D)

where the first summand in the objective minimizes the residual error w.r.t. the generative model (
2
) (
\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29006
are given weights), and the second term provides an
\mathchar 352_{\mathchar 28722}
regularization (
a.k.a.

Tikhonov regularization

[
41
]
) of the shape coefficients
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}

(controlled by the user-specified parameter
\mathchar 28949\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720
).
Note that for mathematical convenience we replaced the constraint
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12850\mathchar 28673_{\mathchar 29003}
with
the constraint
{\mathbf{\mathchar 28721}}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
, where
{\mathbf{\mathchar 28721}}
is a vector with all entries equal to 1;
in other words, we force the shape coefficients to sum-up to 1 but allow them to be negative.

Note that
the regularization term serves the dual purpose of penalizing the occurrence of large negative entries in
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
and
keeping the problem well-posed regardless of
the number of shapes in the library.

From the probabilistic standpoint, problem (
3D-3D
) is a
maximum a posteriori

estimator assuming that the keypoints measurement noise follows a zero-mean Gaussian with covariance
{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}}}{\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}}_{\mathchar 28723}
(where
{\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}}_{\mathchar 28723}
is the 3-by-3 identity matrix) and we have a zero-mean Gaussian prior with covariance
{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28949}}
over the shape parameters
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
(see App.
I
in
[
34
]
).
Problem (
3D-3D
) is non-convex due to the product between
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
in the objective, and due to the nonconvexity of the constraint set
\mathrm{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785
the rotation
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}

is required to belong to, see
e.g.,

[
42
]
. Therefore, existing approaches based on
local search
[
43
,
44
,
45
]
are prone to converge to local minima corresponding to incorrect estimates.

3D-3D solver overview.

We develop the first certifiably optimal algorithm to solve (
3D-3D
).
Towards this goal we show that
(i) the translation
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}
in (
3D-3D
) can be solved in closed form given the
rotation and shape parameters (Section
VI-A1
),
(ii) the shape parameters
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
can be solved in closed form given the rotation (Section
VI-A2
),
and (iii) the rotation can be estimated (independently on shape and translation) using a
tight semidefinite relaxation (Section
VI-A3
).

VI-A
1
Closed-form Translation Estimation

By inspection of (
3D-3D
), we observe that
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}
is unconstrained and appears quadratically in the cost.
Hence, for any choice of
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
, the optimal translation
can be computed in closed-form:

where

\displaystyle\bar{{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}}_{\mathchar 29033}\triangleq\sqrt{\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}_{\mathchar 29047}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\ \ \bar{{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\triangleq\sqrt{\mathchar 29047_{\mathchar 29033}}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29047}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\min_{\begin{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}{\mathchar29027}{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 12850\mathrm{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785\\

\displaystyle\!\!\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29006}\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\bar{{\bm{\mathchar 29049}}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29035}\bar{{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28949\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}

(30)

We can further simplify the problem by adopting the following matrix notations:

Proposition 18 (Optimal Rotation).

The category-level rotation estimation problem (

\displaystyle\min_{\tilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29042}}}\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720}}}

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29042}}}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}{\bm{\mathchar 29009}}\tilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29042}}}

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29042}}}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathchar 29033}\tilde{{\bm{\mathchar 29042}}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 568\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28725

While a QCQP is still a non-convex problem, it admits a standard semidefinite relaxation, described below.

Corollary 19 (Shor’s Semidefinite Relaxation).

The following semidefinite program (SDP) is a convex relaxation of (
43
):

\displaystyle\min_{{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720}}

(44)

s.t.

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathchar 28720}{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathchar 29033}{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 568\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28725

Moreover, when the optimal solution
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}
of (

) has rank 1, it can
be factorized as
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 67482370\begin{array}[]{c}\mathchar 28721\\
\mathrm{\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29027}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 84054785\end{array}\right\delimiter 84267779\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28721\;\mathrm{\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29027}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84267779
where
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8511}

is the optimal rotation minimizing (
37

The relaxation entails solving a small SDP (
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 8706\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
matrix size, and
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28726
linear equality constraints), hence it can be solved in milliseconds
using standard interior-point methods (
e.g.,

[
47
]
).
Similar to related quadratic problems over
\mathrm{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785

[

,
7
,
48
,
49

50
]
, the relaxation (

— solutions in common problems.
Even when the relaxation is not tight, the relaxation allows computing an estimate and a bound on its suboptimality
(see App.
L
in
[
34

).
The proposed solution falls in the class of
certifiable algorithms
(see
[
51

and Appendix A in
[

), since it allows solving a hard (non-convex) problem efficiently and with
provable a posteriori guarantees.

VI-A
4
Summary

The results in this section suggest a simple algorithm to compute a certifiably optimal solution to the
pose and shape estimation problem (
3D-3D
):
(i) we first estimate the rotation
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8511}
using the semidefinite relaxation (
44
)
(and compute the corresponding suboptimality gap
\mathchar 28945
as described in App.
L
in
[
34
]
);
(ii) we retrieve the optimal shape
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
given the
optimal rotation using (
34
);
(iii) we retrieve the optimal translation
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
using (
27
).
If
\mathchar 28945\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
, we certify that
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}}\mathchar 24891\hat{{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}}\delimiter 84054785
is a globally optimal solution to (
3D-3D
).
We call the resulting algorithm
PACE3D
⋆
(
Pose and shApe estimation for 3D-3D Category-level pErception
).

VI-B

We now show how to solve Problem

In fact, even if

\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}\delimiter 84054785

To circumvent the difficulty of fractional programming caused by the geometric reprojection error, we adopt an

algebraic

i.e.,

{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29003}\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29035}{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8235{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}

,
56
]
to solve (
47
) to certifiable global optimality.

Corollary 20 (Order-2 Lasserre’s Moment Relaxation).

The following multi-block SDP is a convex relaxation of (
43
):

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}{\mathchar29027}{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785\\
\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28720}}\mathchar 8706\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}}\mathchar 8706\dots\mathchar 8706\mathcal{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}}\end{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}}

(49)

s.t.

\displaystyle{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 24891\quad{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720

where
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28720}
is the
moment matrix
of size
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 67273472\begin{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}{\mathchar29027}\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28721\\
\mathchar 28722\end{\mathchar29043\mathchar29045\mathchar29026\mathchar29025\mathchar29042\mathchar29042\mathchar29025\mathchar29049}\right\delimiter 84054785
,

{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
are the so called
localizing matrices
(of size
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28720}
for
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28721
)
arising from the inequality constraints
\mathchar 29031_{\mathchar 29034}
in (
48
) and the additional constraint
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28721
,
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720
indicates each element of
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}
,
i.e.,

{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721
, is positive semidefinite,
{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}_{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 84054785
are known matrices with
{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349{\mathbf{\mathchar 28720}}
for
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28721
, and
{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}

collects all linear equality constraints on
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}
(each scalar constraint is written as
\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720}^{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\left\delimiter 0{\bm{\mathchar 28993}}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29034}
for
\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037
).

Moreover, when the optimal solution
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}
of (
49
) is such that

{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28720}
has rank 1, then
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28720}
can be factorized as
{\bm{\mathchar 29016}}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
, where
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathrm{\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29027}\left\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\mathchar 24891\delimiter 67273472{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\delimiter 84267779^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
is a globally optimal for (
47
), and

\delimiter 67482370{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}
denotes the vector of monomials in the entries of
{\bm{\mathchar 29048}}^{\mathchar 8511}
of degree up to 2.

We refer the interested reader to
[
57
, Section 2]
for details about how to generate
\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}\mathchar 24891{\bm{\mathchar 28995}}\delimiter 84054785
from the POP formulation (
48
). In practice, there exists an efficient Matlab implementation
4
4

4

https://github.com/MIT-SPARK/CertifiablyRobustPerception
that automatically generates the SDP relaxation (
49
) given a POP (
48
).
The reader can observe that the SDP (
49
) is conceptually the same as the SDP (
44
) except that (
49
) has more than one positive semidefinite matrix decision variable.
App.
N
in
[
34
]
contains more details about Lasserre’s hierarchy optimality certificates and
the rounding procedure to extract an estimate from the solution of the SDP (
49
).

VI-B
3
Summary

We solve problem (
2D-3D
) by (i) solving the SDP (
49
) using MOSEK
[
47
]
and obtaining
an estimate
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}}\delimiter 84054785
and the corresponding suboptimality gap
\mathchar 28945
, using the rounding procedure in App.
N
in
[
34
]
;
then (ii) we compute
\hat{{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}}
from (
46
).
If
\mathchar 28945\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
, we certify that
\delimiter 67273472\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}}\mathchar 24891\hat{{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}}\mathchar 24891\widehat{{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}}\delimiter 84054785
is a globally optimal solution to (
2D-3D
).
We call this approach
PACE2D
⋆
(
Pose and shApe estimation for 2D-3D Category-level pErception
).

Remark 21 (Distribution of
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
).

The active shape model allows the entries of
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
to be scalars in
\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84267779
, which enables the model to interpolate between shapes; see Fig.
1
.
We note that if the vector
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
is assumed to be a one-hot vector (
i.e.,
it has a single entry equal to 1 and all other entries equal to zero), then there exist trivial solvers for (
2D-3D
) and (
3D-3D
). Namely, one can run
\mathchar 29003
times an instance-based solver (
e.g.,
PnP or 3D registration), once for each shape in the CAD library, and then pick the solution attaining the lowest cost.
Such an approach sacrifices the interpolation power of the active shape model and in the experiments
we show that it leads to large errors when
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
is not a one-hot vector.

VII
Stage II (continued): Further Robustness Through Graduated Non-Convexity

While in principle we can use
PACE3D
⋆
and
PACE2D
⋆
directly after Stage 1,
the measurements selected by
ROBIN
potentially still contain a few outliers.
These outliers could still hinder the quality of the pose and shape estimates.

To compute accurate estimates in the face of those remaining outliers,
we add a robust loss function —in particular, a truncated least square loss— to problems (
3D-3D
) and (
2D-3D
),
and solve the resulting optimization using a standard graduated non-convexity (GNC)
[
58
,
29
]
approach.
At each iteration, GNC alternates between solving a weighted least squares problem in the form (
3D-3D
) and (
2D-3D
) (these can be solved to certifiable optimality using
PACE3D
⋆
and
PACE2D
⋆
) and updating the weights for each measurement (which can be computed in closed form
[
29
]
).
The interested reader can find more details in App.
O
in
[
34
]
.

We release the proposed solvers at
https://github.com/MIT-SPARK/PACE
.

VIII
Experiments

This section presents a comprehensive evaluations of the proposed approaches.
First, we showcase the optimality of
PACE3D
⋆
and robustness of
PACE3D#
through experiments on synthetic data,
PASCAL3D+

[
59
]
and
KeypointNet

[
60
]
(Section
VIII-A
).
Then, we demonstrate the optimality of
PACE2D
⋆
and robustness of
PACE2D#
through experiments on synthetic datasets (Section
VIII-B
).
Finally, we test both
PACE3D#
and
PACE2D#
on
ApolloScape
, a self-driving dataset
[
61
]
.

Experiments in Section
VIII-A
and
VIII-C
are run with an Intel i9-9920X CPU at 3.5 GHz with 128 GB RAM.
Section
VIII-B
experiments are run on MIT SuperCloud
[
62
]
Xeon Platinum 8260 cluster, using 6 threads and 24 GB RAM per run.

VIII-A

Optimality and Robustness of
PACE3D
⋆
and
PACE3D#

Optimality of
PACE3D
⋆
.
To evaluate the performance of
PACE3D
⋆
in solving the outlier-free problem (
3D-3D
), we randomly simulate
\mathchar 29003
shape models
{\cal\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 29035}
whose points
{\bm{\mathchar 29026}}^{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathchar 29033}
’s
are drawn from an i.i.d. Gaussian distribution
{\cal\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472{\mathbf{\mathchar 28720}}\mathchar 24891{\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}}_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785
. We sample
shape parameters
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
uniformly at random in
\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84267779^{\mathchar 29003}
,
and normalize
{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}
such that
{\mathbf{\mathchar 28721}}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}{\bm{\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
. Then we
uniformly sample
{\bm{\mathchar 29010}}
from
\mathrm{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28723\delimiter 84054785
and
{\bm{\mathchar 29044}}
from
{\cal\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472{\mathbf{\mathchar 28720}}\mathchar 24891{\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}}_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785

and generate the measurements
{\bm{\mathchar 29040}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
according to the model (
2
), where the noise
{\bm{\mathchar 28943}}_{\text{\scriptsize{3D}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033}
follows
{\cal\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472{\mathbf{\mathchar 28720}}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28955^{\mathchar 28722}{\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}}_{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785
with standard deviation
\mathchar 28955\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28721
. We fix
\mathchar 29006\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
, and increase
\mathchar 29003
from
\mathchar 28721
up to
[formula]
, to stress-test the algorithms.
We set the regularization factor
\mathchar 28949\mathchar 12349\sqrt{\mathchar 29003\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 29006}
so that larger regularization is imposed when the problem becomes more ill-posed. We compare
PACE3D
⋆
with a baseline approach based on alternating minimization
[
43
,
44
,
45
]
(details in App.
M
in
[
34
]
) that offers no optimality guarantees (label:
